a:T71e,The paper has three methodological parts.

First, a controlled measurement study. The authors benchmark nine production tokenizers and evaluate five languages that have standardized Cyrillic and Latin representations: Ukrainian, Serbian, Kazakh, Azerbaijani, and Moldovan, totaling 8.37 million word forms. They use corpus-level fertility and define Ukrainian overhead relative to English as R(t)=F(t,BrUK)/F(t,Brown). The cross-script setup is important: comparing the same language in Cyrillic versus Latin helps isolate tokenizer-script mismatch from semantic or corpus-content differences.

Second, tokenizer retraining rather than post-hoc vocabulary patching. The paper argues that modern tokenizers already contain Cyrillic characters, so the bottleneck is not character absence but insufficient higher-order merges. To test this directly, they train balanced byte-level BPE tokenizers with different English-Ukrainian allocations under a fixed 200K vocabulary budget. The mechanism is straightforward: if Ukrainian appears often enough during tokenizer training, BPE can learn longer and more reusable Cyrillic subwords, reducing fragmentation. The best balanced tokenizer converges at 158,184 actual entries, which also suggests that effective allocation matters more than simply pushing vocabulary size upward.

Third, inference-time mitigation via prompt compression. They evaluate LLMLingua-2 because its XLM-R backbone is multilingual and can be applied to Ukrainian without language-specific fine-tuning. This is framed correctly as a mitigation, not a root-cause fix. The paper also includes a romanization comparison, not as a policy proposal but as a diagnostic: if Latinized Ukrainian is not consistently cheaper in a controlled setting, then UTF-8 byte length alone cannot explain the observed overhead.b:T68a,There are four results worth retaining.

First, the production-tokenizer benchmark. Across nine commercial tokenizers, Ukrainian incurs 68% to 121% token overhead relative to English on modern tokenizers, and 220% on the older cl100k. This shows the issue is broad rather than model-specific.

Second, the cross-linguistic script comparison. For the five languages with standardized Cyrillic and Latin forms, the overhead tracks the script that is dominant in web usage and, by implication, in tokenizer training data. That pattern supports the training-allocation explanation more than a pure Unicode-byte explanation.

Third, balanced tokenizer retraining. With a 200K vocabulary cap, a balanced English-Ukrainian tokenizer reduces the UK/EN ratio from 2.22x to 1.30x, with the best model converging at 158,184 actual entries and reported significance at p < 0.0001. This is the most actionable result in the paper.

Fourth, a production-like benchmark on 1,536 real e-commerce products with deterministic evaluation. The full paper uses this section to show that corpus-level fertility differences propagate into practical prompt budgets, and that prompt compression and tokenizer retraining are complementary. The abstract excerpt does not expose all task-level numbers, but the role of this experiment is clear: it validates that the tokenization gap is operational, not merely corpus-analytic.

One careful detail deserves mention: the paper reports a negative association between Cyrillic vocabulary allocation and overhead (Spearman rho = -0.536), but it is not statistically significant (p = 0.215, n = 7). The direction is plausible; the evidence is still limited.c:T41f,This is a skim paper unless you work directly on multilingual tokenization. Start with the metric definitions: fertility, R(t), Delta_rom, and Delta_cyr. Those choices determine whether the later claims are interpretable. Then read the cross-script design carefully, because that is the paper’s main control against semantic confounds.

The balanced tokenizer section is the part worth reading closely. It answers why retraining the tokenizer may be more effective than patching an existing vocabulary. If you care about multilingual pretraining, this section is the practical core: fixed vocabulary budget, changed language allocation, measurable change in token efficiency.

The prompt-compression and e-commerce sections can be read more selectively. Their value is in connecting tokenizer inefficiency to real system cost and context loss, not in introducing a new compression method. A focused read takes about 25 to 35 minutes. If you plan to reproduce or extend the work, spend the extra time on tokenizer training details and corpus composition.d:T467,The proposed method is likelihood-array regression, or LAR. For each target token, a fixed scoring language model evaluates the token under a sequence of nested left-context windows rather than only under the full prefix. These evaluations form a structured array indexed by context length and relative token position, with additional features derived from the model’s predictive distribution. Arrays from documents of different lengths are aligned onto a common domain. LAR-1 learns additive contributions from individual cells to the class log odds and aggregates them over tokens and context lengths. LAR-2 adds within-path second-order features for the same target token, including same-context quadratic terms and products across different context lengths. Basis expansions and random squared projections produce finite-dimensional representations, which are estimated with ridge logistic regression. The theory analyzes the within-path quadratic model, decomposes finite-estimator error into approximation, regularization, estimation, and squared-projection components, and connects prediction error to AUC convergence.e:T863,The method has two main components. The first derives Muon’s update direction from a constrained response model. The weight update is posed as a response of the parameter matrix to an applied force, with an output-side safety budget limiting how much the update may perturb the model’s outputs. Among all admissible responses, the preferred one is the maximally dissipative direction. Under the paper’s assumptions, this leads to the orthogonal polar factor of the momentum matrix, which is exactly the semi-orthogonalized direction used by Muon. This is a matrix-level geometric argument, not a coordinate-wise adaptive scaling argument. That is the key distinction from AdamW: AdamW relies on per-parameter second-moment preconditioning, while Muon chooses an update shape that is better behaved in spectral terms and less dominated by a few singular directions.

The second component derives a two-timescale momentum from stress relaxation. Standard momentum assumes a single exponential decay, which corresponds to a single relaxation mode. The paper argues that this is too crude for real training dynamics. Borrowing from Maxwell-type viscoelastic models, it decomposes internal stress into a fast and a slow component, each tracked by its own EMA coefficient, and combines them into the momentum that is later semi-orthogonalized. This yields the Bi-Maxwell optimizer. Importantly, the rest of Muon is kept unchanged: the orthogonalization step stays, and the benchmark recipe is otherwise held fixed. That makes the causal claim cleaner, because the observed gains can be attributed mainly to the memory structure rather than to a bundle of recipe changes.

A central conceptual point is that the optimal memory length should not be constant over training. Early on, gradient directions change quickly, so long memory mixes in stale directions and hurts responsiveness. Later, directions evolve more slowly, so longer memory improves denoising and accumulates consistent signal. Bi-Maxwell does not explicitly schedule beta over time; instead, it uses two fixed timescales to approximate a training-dependent effective memory length.f:T773,The headline result is on the public modded-nanogpt optimizer speedrun benchmark. Replacing Muon’s single-timescale momentum with the Bi-Maxwell memory reaches the target loss at step 2635, improving on the standing public record of 2690 steps at the time. The paper also reports a 40-step improvement over a bare tuned-Muon baseline without extra add-ons such as SOAP. The absolute gain is modest, but on a benchmark that has already been heavily tuned by the community, a further improvement from changing only the momentum structure is meaningful.

The second important result is a read-only measurement study over 8 independent training trajectories, aimed at estimating the optimal momentum memory length during training. The measured trend matches the theory: the preferred memory is shorter early in training and becomes longer later. The value of this experiment is less about the exact numbers and more about turning a usually opaque hyperparameter into something that can be observed and discussed over the course of optimization.

The paper also makes a point about evaluation discipline. The comparison is not against a weak in-house baseline, but against a public standing record on a benchmark that has been tuned adversarially over time. Since the rest of Muon is held fixed and only the memory mechanism is changed, the attribution is tighter than in many optimizer papers where gains can be confounded by recipe drift.

At least from the provided full text, the direct empirical validation is still centered on the public small-scale speedrun setup. The paper cites prior evidence that Muon itself has scaled to billion-parameter and trillion-token settings, but the new Bi-Maxwell variant is not yet shown in a full large-scale pretraining run here. So the empirical status is best read as theory-driven validation on a strong public benchmark, not yet a finalized frontier-scale recipe.10:T68d,Start with the Introduction to understand the framing: the paper is not merely rebranding momentum, but assigning distinct physical meanings to update direction and memory. Then focus on Section 3, especially the part around stress relaxation and memory length. That is where the paper’s real novelty sits. While reading, keep two questions in mind. First, what exactly is constrained by the output-side safety budget, and why does that lead to the polar-factor or semi-orthogonalized direction? Second, why is a single EMA equivalent to an overly restrictive single-mode relaxation assumption, and why is a two-timescale model the minimal useful extension?

In the experiments, prioritize the main benchmark result and the plots measuring optimal memory length over training. The former answers whether the method helps; the latter answers whether the theory is reflected in data. If time is limited, it is fine to postpone judging every physical analogy literally and instead inspect which assumptions are essential for the derivation and which are mainly there to obtain a tractable form. The appendix discussion on assumption boundaries and relaxations is worth reading because it determines how far this framework might generalize beyond the exact setting studied here.

Two follow-up questions are especially worth carrying into a deeper read. One is whether Bi-Maxwell should be extended to richer relaxation spectra, possibly continuous or layer-specific. The other is whether the optimal memory length could be estimated online and used to adapt beta directly, instead of approximating it with two fixed EMAs. The paper opens both directions but does not close them.11:T6cb,The proposed method is Adaptive Log-Space (AL) quantization, embedded in a broader analysis of optimizer-state heterogeneity. The authors first observe that many non-negative optimizer states, especially second moments and confidence accumulators, are heavy-tailed, vary in dynamic range across blocks, and often contain exact zeros. Standard blockwise linear quantization can collapse small positive values to zero, which destroys the distinction between dormant state and weak but nonzero history. AL addresses this by fitting a logarithmic interval to the positive values within each block while reserving code zero exclusively for exact zero, yielding the invariant q = 0 if and only if x = 0. This preserves true zeros and prevents positive second moments from being quantized away into zero. The design is not a single uniform low-bit policy. Non-negative states can use AL8 or AL16, signed momentum is encoded separately, and CAME confidence states may use higher precision than second moments. A second key idea is topology-aware grouping. The paper shows that quantization blocks should align with the optimizer’s state topology: cutting across Adafactor row/column factors or mixing unrelated statistics in one block can amplify update error. The study covers four optimizer paths—dense AdamW, factored Adafactor, confidence-guided CAME, and projected-state APOLLO—and evaluates not only state reconstruction but also update-domain fidelity, temporal drift, and error propagation through inverse square roots, factor reconstruction, confidence modulation, and projected scaling. The implementation includes both a Python reference path and fused CUDA kernels, with separate checks for algorithmic and numerical consistency.12:T5a3,The experiments are organized into controlled diagnostics and end-to-end pretraining runs. In the diagnostic part, the authors trace optimizer states from language-model pretraining and compare their value ranges, exact-zero patterns, and temporal behavior. They then evaluate how different quantization schemes affect both state reconstruction and update error. The conclusions are deliberately nuanced rather than universal. For dense AdamW, AL8 is competitive with the evaluated 8-bit AdamW baseline. For CAME, confidence states are more precision-sensitive than second moments, so using too low a precision there hurts update fidelity more noticeably. For Adafactor, protecting sensitive tensors and matching quantization granularity to row/column factor topology is materially better than coarse uniform blocking. For APOLLO, projected-space states exhibit yet another error-propagation pattern, reinforcing the point that they should not be treated as ordinary dense arrays. The end-to-end validation uses 20K-step TinyLlama-1.1B pretraining and 100K-step GPT-2 pretraining. The paper’s overall conclusion is bounded: AL8 can match existing 8-bit baselines on several paths, but a single low-precision recipe is not sufficient for all structured optimizer states. The available text does not expose the full final-loss tables, so the most useful takeaway is the pattern of sensitivity and failure modes rather than one headline metric.13:T49b,Start with the introduction and the figure that contrasts the four optimizer-state topologies. That framing is essential; without it, the rest can look like a minor quantization tweak. Then focus on the paper’s three analysis axes: representation, topology, and update semantics. The most informative experiments are the ones that translate state error into update error, because they explain why similar reconstruction quality can still lead to different training outcomes. After that, read the AL representation details, especially the exact-zero invariant and the motivation for log-space coding of non-negative EMA states. In the experiments, prioritize the ablations on block granularity, parameter grouping, and confidence-state precision, then read the TinyLlama-1.1B and GPT-2 pretraining sections. For a quick skim, keep three points: structured optimizer states cannot be handled like dense Adam states, CAME confidence states are a distinct sensitivity point, and quantization groups should respect state topology. A focused read should take around 30–45 minutes; reproducing the work would require a closer look at the implementation and CUDA-validation sections.14:T49c,The paper formulates validation as a linear inverse problem. The unknown reliability surface is f. Each validation intervention v_j observes an integral <v_j, f>. A deployment choice k asks for <k, f>. The central theorem states that, over all bounded reliability functions 0≤f≤1, the deployment target is exactly identifiable from the validation evidence if and only if k lies in the span of the validation kernels. Otherwise, the exact worst-case ambiguity equals the L1 distance from k to that span, called the blind residual. This is an attainable ambiguity, not merely a loose bound. The paper then treats post-evidence target selection: if the deployment target is chosen after seeing validation results, pointwise intervals are insufficient; validity for arbitrary selectors is equivalent to simultaneous coverage over the whole target family. For iid best-of-N, score percentiles U are uniform and selecting the maximum among N candidates gives the kernel k_N(u)=N u^{N-1}. Auditing best-of-n for n=1,...,m spans polynomials of degree m-1, so certifying a larger N becomes a bounded Hausdorff moment problem over increasingly tail-concentrated order-statistic kernels.15:Ta03,技术主线很完整，可以分成诊断、推导、修正三步。

第一步是诊断 solver coupling。作者先写清楚标准 self-conditioning recurrence：每一步模型接收当前 latent z_k、时间 t_k，以及上一时刻 clean prediction 作为 self-conditioning 输入 m_k。训练时，m 是由同一个当前 z 通过一条 stop-gradient 分支得到；推理时，m_k = p_{k-1}，但此时 z_k 已经经过 solver 用 p_{k-1} 更新过。于是同一份信息通过两条路径进入模型：一条隐式地在 latent 里，一条显式地在 self-conditioning 输入里。这个双通道重复在小步长时还不太严重，但 few-step 时每步跨度大，solver 折叠更强，冗余迅速放大。

第二步是把这个失配拆成两个可修正的后果。其一是 self-conditioning redundancy。作者利用冻结模型的投影权重，分析 latent 输入通道和 self-conditioning 输入通道在表示空间中的共享方向。通过对相关矩阵或投影结构做特征分解，识别出两条路径重叠最强的 eigendirections，然后在采样时对 self-conditioning 输入沿这些方向做抑制，让它更多提供 latent 尚未携带的互补信息。这里的关键不是简单缩放整个 self-conditioning，而是按几何方向有选择地 damp 掉冗余分量，所以叫 untied：不再把 solver 中使用的预测和 self-conditioning 中使用的预测视为同一个对象。

其二是 solver approximation gap。标准 solver 在一步开始时只用单点预测 p_k 去近似该步积分所需的平均预测，但真实需要的是区间上的平均量，这个误差是一阶依赖于步长的。作者用 prediction history 去近似这个 step-average prediction，并用离线轨迹统计来定标修正尺度。重要的是，修正后的 prediction 只喂给 solver 做 latent update，而原始 prediction 仍保留给下一步 self-conditioning。这样就把“给 solver 的量”和“给模型自条件的量”解耦了。

第三步是组装成 Untied Self-Conditioning。它是一个 training-free sampler，每步仍只需一次模型前向，不增加 NFE。流程上：模型输出当前 clean prediction；原始预测缓存为下一步 self-conditioning；同时对其做两类修正，一类是基于投影几何的冗余抑制，另一类是基于历史的 solver 平均预测校正；最后用修正后的量更新 latent。这个设计很克制，没有引入额外网络，也没有做 test-time optimization，而是直接利用现有模型结构和采样轨迹信息。16:T97e,The method unfolds in three stages: diagnosis, derivation, and correction.

First, the paper diagnoses solver coupling in the standard self-conditioning recurrence. At step k, the model takes the current latent z_k, time t_k, and a self-conditioning input m_k derived from the previous clean prediction. In training, m is obtained from the same current noisy state through a stop-gradient branch, so there is no solver update between z and m. In sampling, however, m_k = p_{k-1} while z_k has already been updated using p_{k-1}. The same information therefore enters the model twice: implicitly through the latent and explicitly through the self-conditioning input. With small steps this is limited; with few large steps it becomes substantial.

Second, the authors decompose the mismatch into two correctable consequences. The first is self-conditioning redundancy. Using the frozen projection weights of the trained model, they analyze which directions in representation space are shared by the latent-input pathway and the self-conditioning pathway. An eigendecomposition identifies directions where the explicit self-conditioning signal is most redundant with information already embedded in the latent. At inference time, those directions are damped so that the self-conditioning input contributes complementary information rather than repeating what the latent already carries. This is a directional correction, not a global rescaling.

The second consequence is a solver approximation gap. A standard solver uses a single-point prediction at the start of the step to approximate the average prediction needed for the exact integral over that interval. The authors show that this gap is first-order in step width. They then approximate the step-average prediction from prediction history, with the correction scale calibrated using offline trajectory statistics. Crucially, the corrected prediction is used only for the solver update, while the raw prediction is stored as the self-conditioning input for the next step.

These two ideas are combined into Untied Self-Conditioning, a training-free sampler that still uses one model evaluation per step. The prediction used for the solver and the prediction used for self-conditioning are no longer tied to be the same object. The design is restrained: no extra network, no retraining, no additional NFE, only a better use of the model’s own geometry and trajectory history.17:T6d9,The empirical case rests on three kinds of evidence.

First, the headline generation results are strong. On both ELF and LangFlow, Untied Self-Conditioning improves quality at every tested NFE, with the largest gains in the low-step regime. The abstract reports that at 8 model evaluations, LangFlow reduces GenPPL on OpenWebText from 531 to 62, an 8.6x improvement, and on ELF-B from 71 to 43. At 32 evaluations on ELF-B, it reaches GenPPL 21, approaching the data reference, while retaining 95% throughput. These numbers matter because they show better quality at essentially the same compute budget.

Second, the paper includes a controlled coupling experiment. It varies only the dependence between successive steps while keeping each step’s marginal prediction distribution fixed. As coupling increases, the benefit of self-conditioning shrinks monotonically, and under strong coupling full self-conditioning becomes harmful. This is one of the most convincing parts of the paper because it isolates the mechanism: the issue is not simply that single-step predictions become worse in few-step sampling, but that solver-induced cross-step coupling makes the explicit self-conditioning pathway redundant or counterproductive.

Third, the ablations separate the two corrections: redundancy damping and solver-average correction. Both help, and their effects compound in the few-step regime. One mainly improves the quality of the self-conditioning signal; the other improves the numerical update used by the solver. The paper also emphasizes that the gains do not come from extra forward passes. When reading the experiments closely, the most informative pieces are the NFE-quality curves, the controlled coupling plots, and the component ablations.18:T547,This one deserves a close read. Start with Section 2 to make sure the standard self-conditioning recurrence and the train–inference discrepancy are fully clear. Then spend most of the time on Section 3, which is the core of the paper: it derives the redundancy problem and the solver approximation gap separately. If that section is understood, the main contribution is understood. Section 4 is shorter and mainly assembles the final sampler. In Section 5, focus on three parts: the few-step headline results, the controlled coupling experiment, and the component ablations. The most important figures are the mismatch schematic, the projection/eigendirection analysis, and the GenPPL-versus-NFE curves.

Three follow-up questions are worth keeping in mind. First, the geometric correction based on frozen projection weights may generalize to other diffusion or flow language models, and perhaps even to test-time corrections in AR models. Second, the solver correction uses offline trajectory statistics, which raises the question of how model- or sampler-specific the calibration is and whether it can be made adaptive. Third, although the method is inference-time only, it exposes a training deficiency: if training explicitly simulated solver-induced coupling, would the model learn a more robust self-conditioning representation from the start?19:T9b2,方法分成理论拆解和算法设计两部分。

理论部分，作者先区分 mass coverage、output fidelity、task utility 三层目标。对 mass coverage，他们指出阈值计数 k(epsilon) 只是在问“有多少权重大于某阈值”，但压缩预算真正关心的是“要保留多少 token 才能覆盖 90% 之类的总概率质量”。决定后者的不是局部亮点，而是全局 score-gap profile，也就是排序后 logit 的衰减几何。论文给出一组 all-rank gap 条件：如果 gap envelope 在 log-rank 意义下斜率大于 1，tail 可求和，固定质量覆盖所需 budget 可以与长度无关；如果斜率低于 1，budget 会随长度增长。作者还用 Gaussian logits 说明一个反直觉点：即便看起来有“校准后的少量大权重”，要覆盖 90% mass 仍可能需要接近 0.61n 的 token，压缩空间并不大。

对 output fidelity，作者指出遗漏 mass 本身不是误差证书，关键在于被丢弃 value 的加权和是否能被恢复或近似。对一个固定 attention row，遗漏部分的 weighted sum 是精确的缺失统计量。进一步，如果希望一个线性 sketch 同时恢复完整输出和任意保留子预算下的输出，那么除了保留的真实 token 外，再加一个 tail aggregate 就达到匹配的维度下界。这给后面的算法设计提供了结构性依据：不是只保留 top-k token，而是要显式存一个 tail summary slot。

对 task utility，作者构造了 retrieval–aggregation 的 planted-token 模型，说明 exact attention 并不总是最适合任务目标。对 retrieval-like 任务，renormalized top-k 有时会因为压掉 diffuse noisy bulk 而优于 exact attention；但对 aggregation 任务，保留完整 softmax 更合理。也就是说，“更接近原 attention”不自动等于“更接近任务最优”。

基于这些分析，作者提出 CertKV。它是 training-free 的 KV compressor。设计上每个 head 预留一个 tail-summary slot，其余预算从 observation window 中选真实 token；分配策略不只看 attention score，还看 value dispersion，也就是尾部 value 是否容易被一个聚合向量概括。被省略的尾部用加权 key-value summary 存起来，且 synthetic slot 与真实 token 占用同样的物理预算。这个设计和很多只做 token selection 的方法不同，核心差异在于它承认“尾部不是纯噪声”，而是用一个显式统计量去补偿。1a:T995,The paper has two layers: a theoretical decomposition and a practical compressor.

On mass coverage, the authors argue that threshold counts are the wrong primitive. Counting weights above epsilon does not answer the cache-budget question, which is how many tokens are needed to retain, say, 90% of the softmax mass. That quantity is governed by the global score-gap profile after sorting logits, not by a few bright entries. They derive complementary all-rank gap conditions: if the log-rank gap envelope has slope above one, the tail is summable and a fixed-mass budget can remain length-independent; if the slope is below one, the required budget grows with sequence length. A Gaussian-logit example makes the point concrete: one can observe a seemingly sparse calibrated count while still needing roughly 61% of tokens to retain 90% of the mass.

On output fidelity, the paper shows that omitted mass alone is not an error certificate. What matters is the weighted sum of omitted values. For a fixed realized attention row, that omitted weighted value sum is the exact missing statistic. Under a stronger linear-sketch requirement—recovering both the full output and every retained sub-budget—the authors show that keeping the selected values plus one tail aggregate achieves the matching dimension bound. This is the structural motivation for the algorithmic design: a compressor should not only keep top tokens, but also maintain an explicit tail summary.

On task utility, the paper introduces a planted retrieval–aggregation model. It shows that exact attention is not always the right target. For retrieval-like tasks, renormalized top-k can outperform exact attention by suppressing diffuse noisy mass, while for aggregation tasks exact attention remains preferable. So preserving the original attention output is not automatically the same as preserving task performance.

These analyses motivate CertKV, a training-free KV compressor. CertKV reserves one tail-summary slot per head, selects real tokens from an observation window, and allocates more token budget to heads whose omitted values are harder to summarize, measured through value dispersion. The omitted tail is stored as a weighted key-value summary, and the synthetic slot counts against the same physical budget as real tokens. This differs from pure token-selection methods by explicitly modeling the tail as compressed state rather than treating it as disposable noise.1b:T4d5,The evaluation covers all 503 LongBench-v2 examples, three backbones, three compression ratios, multi-length RULER, one-factor studies, and a packed-cache prototype. The headline result is that under the pre-specified four-baseline comparison grid, CertKV is top-two in 7 of 9 LongBench-v2 settings. At 128K context on RULER it stays in the leading tier, and on Llama with 64K context and 10x compression it achieves the intended physical cache reduction.

The more useful part is how the experiments map onto the theory. One set of studies examines score-gap profiles versus mass coverage, showing that sparsity observed at shorter lengths does not reliably extrapolate to longer contexts. Another set tests the benefit of the tail summary for output fidelity, validating that the extra aggregate is not an ad hoc engineering trick but the exact statistic suggested by the analysis. A third controlled retrieval–aggregation setup recovers the predicted transition where renormalized top-k can beat exact attention for retrieval-like targets but not for aggregation. So the paper is not just reporting benchmark wins; it is checking whether the three separations—coverage, fidelity, and utility—actually explain empirical behavior.1c:T927,技术路线分成两步。第一步，作者先研究一个理想化的 offline oracle：如果所有请求的完成长度事先已知，怎样分配到多个 replicas 才能最小化 step-level makespan。分析结论是，在长尾分布下，最优解呈现 tail-isolation-with-balancing 结构：把少量极长请求单独放到一个 tail replica 或 tail pool，其他大多数请求放到 bulk pool，但不是简单按请求数平均分，而是让两个池子的完成时间尽量平衡。作者进一步指出，一个简单的 top-k tail isolation 策略就能逼近大部分 oracle 收益，不必求解复杂的全局 assignment。

第二步，把这个结构在线化，形成 TailSieve。难点在于 rollout 前不知道哪些 prompt 会变成长尾，而且最优的 tail 大小和 replica 划分会随策略更新、长度分布变化、并发度变化而变化。作者利用一个经验事实：同一 prompt group 在相邻 rollout round 中，长度排序具有一定持续性。于是他们先做 partial rollout，在一个 cutoff 时刻观察哪些 prompt groups 仍未完成，把这些 unfinished groups 作为下一轮的高召回 tail 候选。这样得到的是 training-free tail signal，不需要额外训练长度预测器。

在此基础上，系统采用 two-pool routing：
1. bulk pool 处理大多数普通请求，保持较高并发；
2. tail pool 专门处理疑似长尾请求，使用较低并发，避免超长样本把大 batch decode 拖慢。

作者还设计了一个 hierarchical controller 联合调节两个量：tail isolation size 和 replica split。内层控制器根据观测到的完成时间平衡两个池子的结束时刻；外层控制器根据 response-work history 以及实测的 concurrency-throughput model，调节边际容量分配。这里的关键不是静态规则，而是随着 workload 演化在线跟踪最合适的隔离规模和资源占比。

论文特别强调两点边界。第一，它不同于 predictor-based skewness-aware dispatch，因为它不依赖额外长度模型。第二，它不同于异步或 partial-rollout reuse 系统，因为未完成轨迹不会跨 policy update 复用；下一轮只是重新生成这些 tail candidates。因此它保持 on-policy generation，不引入 routing-induced length bias，也不需要处理 policy lag。1d:T990,The method has two layers. First, the authors analyze an offline oracle problem: if all completion lengths were known in advance, how should requests be assigned to replicas to minimize rollout-step makespan? Their main result is that, in the long-tail regime, the optimum has a tail-isolation-with-balancing structure. A small number of extreme-tail requests should be isolated onto a tail replica or tail pool, while the bulk of requests goes to a separate bulk pool. The goal is not to equalize request counts, but to balance completion times across pools. They further show that a simple top-k tail-isolation rule recovers most of the oracle gain, so the useful structure is much simpler than a full combinatorial assignment.

Second, they turn this structure into an online system called TailSieve. The challenge is that response lengths are unknown before generation, and the best tail size and replica split vary over time with policy updates, workload shifts, and concurrency-dependent decoding throughput. The key empirical observation is that prompt groups that are relatively long in one rollout round tend to remain relatively long in the next. TailSieve therefore runs a partial rollout, stops at a cutoff, and treats the unfinished prompt groups as high-recall tail candidates for the next round. This gives a training-free tail signal, avoiding an auxiliary length predictor.

The runtime then uses two-pool routing:
1. a bulk pool for most requests at higher concurrency;
2. a tail pool for likely long requests at lower concurrency, so a few very long generations do not stall large decoding batches.

A hierarchical controller jointly adapts two quantities: the tail isolation size and the replica split. The inner loop balances observed pool completion times, while the outer loop adjusts marginal capacity using response-work history and a measured concurrency-throughput model. The important point is that both workload assignment and hardware allocation are adapted online rather than fixed.

The paper is careful about what it does not do. It is not a predictor-based skewness-aware dispatcher, because it does not train a separate length model. It is also not an asynchronous or partial-rollout reuse system, because unfinished trajectories are not carried across policy updates. Tail candidates are regenerated from scratch under the current policy. That preserves on-policy sampling and avoids routing-induced length bias.1e:T560,From the provided full-text excerpt, the experiments appear in three parts. First is the oracle analysis. The paper shows that, with known lengths, tail isolation with balancing beats uniform routing, and that a simple top-k isolation policy captures most of the oracle gain. Figure 2 is likely the key reference here because it justifies the structural claim rather than presenting a heuristic.

Second is the validation of the tail signal. The authors use unfinished prompt groups from a partial rollout as candidates for the next round, and Section 2.3 plus Appendix B and Figure 3 reportedly show that this signal has high recall. This is central to the method: if tail persistence across rounds were weak, the online approximation would fail.

Third is the end-to-end systems evaluation. TailSieve jointly controls tail workload and replica allocation as the rollout distribution evolves, and the paper claims lower step-level makespan while preserving on-policy generation. The excerpt does not include the full result tables, so exact gains, model sizes, GPU counts, or workload scales cannot be stated reliably. What is clear is that the relevant baselines include uniform routing, predictor-based skewness-aware dispatch, and asynchronous or partial-rollout-style alternatives, and that the main metric is rollout-step completion time rather than per-request latency.1f:T40d,A good reading order is: theory first, online signal second, controller third. Start with Sections 2.1 and 2.2 to understand why the offline optimum has a tail-isolation-with-balancing form instead of simple load balancing by request count. That is the most informative part of the paper. Then read Section 2.3 and the figure on partial-rollout recall to see whether the tail signal is stable enough to support the online design. Finally, read Section 2.4 and the controller figure to understand how isolation size and replica split are jointly adapted.

If your goal is to decide whether this is useful for an RL or OPD stack, focus on three items: the oracle figure, the tail-signal figure, and the main end-to-end table. That should take about 25 to 35 minutes. For a deeper read, inspect two assumptions carefully: whether prompt-group persistence holds under more heterogeneous rollout workloads, and whether the concurrency-throughput model remains stable across different batch shapes, KV-cache pressure, and distributed settings.20:T520,WnW has two coordinated components: offline head triage and online chunk recall. In the offline stage, each KV head is scored along two axes. Voice score measures how strongly the head is grounded in audio rather than other modalities or generic sink behavior. Head sensitivity estimates how much quality is lost when that head’s KV cache is compressed, using a gradient-based sensitivity signal. Based on these two quantities, heads are partitioned into three roles. Anchor heads keep the full audio KV cache on GPU and act as decode-time observers of audio importance. Tidal heads keep only part of their audio KV on GPU while storing the complement on CPU, making those positions recallable. Fixed heads use a static prefill-time subset with no recall path. During decoding, attention from anchor heads is aggregated into chunk-level importance scores over the audio timeline. The highest-scoring chunks are recalled from CPU into the tidal heads’ GPU cache, while chunks that lose relevance are swapped out. The on-GPU cache therefore waxes and wanes with the generation process instead of shrinking monotonically. A key distinction from many text-side methods is that recall is driven by a small set of heads that preserve full temporal access to the audio, rather than by generic similarity heuristics.21:T421,The empirical story has two parts. First, the paper directly measures the mismatch between prefill and decode attention on LibriSpeech-Long with Voxtral. Prefill attention from the first generated token is concentrated near the start of the audio, whereas attention accumulated over the full decode is much more distributed. The top-K overlap between the two rankings is weak, which undermines the premise of prefill-only retention. Second, WnW delivers strong results under tight memory budgets. On two 3B backbones, Voxtral-mini-3b and Qwen2.5-Omni-3B, WnW stays within about 1.6 WER points of full-cache decoding even when only 20% of audio tokens remain on GPU. In the same regime, prefill-only baselines can fail to terminate and produce WER above 100%. The paper also reports generalization across languages, tasks, and domains, and favorable scaling behavior on a 24B backbone relative to other KV-management baselines. Importantly, the CPU-GPU recall overhead is reported to be limited, so the gains are not bought by an impractical runtime penalty.22:T9a8,论文先做了一个很关键的诊断实验：控制 drafter 可见的视觉预算，从无视觉、低预算到全视觉，观察 accept length 的变化。结论不是简单的“视觉越多越好”，而是最优预算随状态变化且明显非单调。作者把 preferred budget 定义为“达到最大 accept length 的最小预算”，而不是最高 token 概率或最低单步延迟。这个定义很合理，因为 speculative decoding 的核心目标是提高端到端吞吐，而 accept length 比局部概率更直接对应系统收益。实验里 25% 视觉预算在 56.0% 的状态上最优，而 full access 只在 2.9% 的状态上最优，说明固定全量视觉输入既浪费算力，也可能干扰草稿质量。

基于这个观察，作者提出 FOVEA。整体结构分三步。第一，先用 target model 的视觉编码器和 projector 对图像编码一次，得到 projected visual tokens，并构建每张图像的外部 visual memory。这样后续 draft 过程不需要反复处理整张图。第二，在 draft-tree 解码时，每个节点的 hidden state 作为 query 去检索这块 memory，选出与当前状态最相关的视觉证据，而且检索数量是动态的，不是固定 top-k。作者强调 dynamic cardinality，因为不同状态需要的证据量不同。第三，检索到的视觉证据不插入自回归 token 序列，而是通过一个 lightweight gated residual module 融合到当前节点的表示里，只影响当前 token scoring。这样做的关键好处是不会改变历史上下文，因此不会破坏已有 KV cache。

这里的方法取舍很清楚。若把检索到的视觉 token 真正拼回上下文，理论上表达能力更强，但历史 cache 会失效，推理加速收益会被重算吞掉。FOVEA 选择“外部记忆 + 当前状态修正”这条路，牺牲一部分深层交互，换取 cache reuse 和系统可实现性。对 speculative decoding 来说，这是合理的工程-算法折中。

从文中描述看，visual memory 至少包含用于检索的 key 表示和用于融合的 value/update 表示，先经 layer norm 和线性投影构建。每个 draft hidden state 对 memory 做相关性检索，得到 state-conditioned visual readout，再经门控残差注入当前表示。门控设计的意义在于：当当前 token 几乎不依赖视觉时，模型可以接近退化为纯文本 drafter；当需要视觉证据时，再有选择地放大视觉修正。23:Ta86,The paper starts with a strong diagnostic. It varies the visual budget available to the drafter, from no visual input to full visual access, and measures accept length. The result is not monotonic: the preferred budget changes by state, and larger visual access is often suboptimal. The authors define preferred budget as the smallest tested budget that achieves the maximum accept length, rather than the budget with the highest token probability or lowest isolated latency. That choice is sensible because speculative decoding is ultimately about end-to-end throughput, and accept length is more directly tied to system gain. In their analysis, a 25% visual budget is optimal for 56.0% of evaluated states, while full access is optimal for only 2.9%, which already undermines the fixed-interface assumption.

Based on this observation, they propose FOVEA. The method has three main steps. First, the image is encoded once by the target-side visual encoder and multimodal projector, producing projected visual tokens that are stored in a reusable per-image visual memory. This avoids repeated full-image processing during drafting. Second, during draft-tree decoding, each draft hidden state acts as a query into that memory and retrieves the visual evidence most relevant to the current state. Importantly, retrieval cardinality is dynamic rather than fixed, because different states need different amounts of evidence. Third, the retrieved evidence is not inserted into the autoregressive token context. Instead, it is fused into the current node representation through a lightweight gated residual module, affecting only current-token scoring.

That design choice is the core systems insight. If the retrieved visual tokens were appended to the context, the historical KV cache would become inconsistent and would need recomputation, erasing much of the speculative speedup. FOVEA instead uses an external memory plus hidden-state correction, preserving cache reuse while still allowing state-conditioned visual adaptation. It is a deliberate trade-off: somewhat less expressive than rewriting the full context, but much more compatible with practical decoding.

From the method description, the visual memory contains at least retrieval-oriented key representations and fusion-oriented value/update representations, built from normalized projected visual tokens through learned projections. Each draft hidden state retrieves a state-conditioned visual readout, which is then injected via a gate. The gate matters because many decoding states are effectively text-dominant; in those cases the drafter should remain close to a text-only model, and only amplify visual correction when needed.24:T5cb,Experiments span three vision-language backbones and nine multimodal benchmarks. The main outcomes are improved draft acceptance and improved end-to-end decoding speed relative to strong baselines, with up to 2.13× speedup over standard autoregressive decoding.

The diagnostic results are arguably even more informative than the headline speed number. Visual-budget sensitivity varies not only across tasks but also across generation stages within the same sample. The effect is especially pronounced on OCR-heavy tasks, where later tokens often need finer-grained visual evidence. This explains why static compressed visual interfaces can work in some phases and fail in others. The paper also shows that high visual budgets are rarely optimal: 75% and full access seldom win. That means excess visual context is not just computationally expensive; it can also hurt drafting quality by adding irrelevant evidence.

The full excerpt does not list every benchmark number, but the mechanism behind the gains is clear. FOVEA improves average accept length, reducing target-model fallback, while also avoiding repeated visual recomputation and cache invalidation. The most important ablations to inspect are likely fixed budget vs dynamic budget, context insertion vs gated residual fusion, with vs without external visual memory, and task-wise budget preference distributions. Those are the experiments that validate the paper’s central claim rather than merely reporting a speedup.25:T418,For a quick pass, start with the visual-budget diagnostic in the introduction. That section establishes whether the paper’s premise is real: if optimal visual access is state-dependent and non-monotonic, then a fixed visual interface is indeed the wrong abstraction. Next, read the method overview carefully, focusing on the interaction among external visual memory, dynamic retrieval cardinality, and gated residual correction, and why this preserves KV-cache reuse. Then inspect the main result tables to verify that gains appear in both acceptance and wall-clock speed, not just one of them. Finally, look at the ablations and OCR/chart-heavy tasks, where on-demand visual evidence should matter the most.

Estimated reading time is about 25 minutes. If the goal is only to understand the systems idea, the diagnostic section, method figure, and main tables are enough. If the goal is to adapt similar mechanisms to training-time multimodal conditioning, spend more time on memory construction, retrieval overhead, and the exact fusion design.26:T41d,SSDi8 combines execution-path engineering with SSD-aware quantization:
- Persistent INT8 path: identify channel-/shape-varying activations that are repeatedly reused across SSD modules; quantize them at carefully chosen points and reuse cached INT8 activations to avoid frequent precision bouncing and DRAM overhead.
- Element-wise/GEMM entanglement: SSD interleaves element-wise multiplications with matrix multiplications, which commonly forces higher-precision intermediates. The paper proposes a sparse-aware reformulation that reorganizes these computations so INT8 kernels can be used end-to-end, and argues correctness via a formal equivalence analysis.
- Exploit SSD’s intrinsic 2D structure: the external model dimension is effectively factored into (num_heads, head_dim) with different outlier behaviors; SSDi8 explicitly leverages this to reduce quantization error instead of using a single coarse scaling scheme.
- Error-mean correction: add a per-channel error-mean correction term to compensate systematic bias and improve PTQ stability.27:T4bf,All models are GPT-2 scale: 12 layers, 12 heads, hidden size 768, trained for 3 epochs on a 1B-token OpenWebText split, for roughly 3B token exposures. As dense language models, softmax variants remain better: sigmoid is worse by about 0.36 PPL with RoPE and about 1.21 PPL without RoPE. The picture changes once learned hard eviction is introduced. Relative to their own no-eviction references, the sigmoid-gated models achieve useful deletion rates with almost no perplexity cost. Sig+G RoPE removes 19.8% of KV entries with essentially unchanged PPL (22.424 vs 22.440), and Sig+G NoPE removes 32.2% with only a small increase (24.637 vs 24.603). Under a matched live-cache protocol, the learned sigmoid-gated models also outperform the paper’s H2O and KeyDiff implementations, whereas softmax-gated models do not. A random support-removal probe is important here: sigmoid attention is not generically more robust to arbitrary KV deletion, and can in fact be more sensitive than softmax at larger random removal rates. That strengthens the paper’s interpretation that the gain comes from better compatibility between learned deletion patterns and the attention substrate, not from generic deletion robustness.28:T652,The methodology rests on three intentionally independent measurement paths. First, the authors build a 64-shape sweep of LLM primitives that preserve semantics while changing expression form, and they record supported-device sets per operation rather than a vague residency score. This is how they establish that placement is a property of expression, not semantics. A representative example is that fused RMSNorm is fully eligible while an equivalent subln decomposition is not admitted to ANE execution. Second, they train matched real models across architecture families, sizes, and precisions. Quantized checkpoints are byte-identical in structure to their fp16 counterparts, so deployment comparisons isolate encoding rather than graph differences. Third, they read ANE memory-controller byte counters during inference, which lets them verify what actually ran and how many bytes were moved, instead of trusting compile-time plans or fragile power telemetry. From these measurements they derive several mechanism-level conclusions. Placement is jointly determined by architecture, size, and encoding rather than parameter count alone. Once resident, decode behaves like streaming the full weight set every token, so latency is largely a function of bytes streamed per token. They also show that heavily grouped GQA, especially with 2 KV heads, is a smaller and more compiler-friendly operator family on ANE than the conv-heavy alternatives examined. This reverses the usual CPU-side intuition: on this accelerator, the advantage comes from grouping and byte economy, not from an abstract preference for attention.29:T5ff,Several results carry most of the paper’s weight. On placement, semantically equivalent formulations can diverge completely: fused RMSNorm is reported as 100% eligible while the decomposed form is 0%, which directly supports the expression-vs-semantics claim. On architecture and encoding, a 22.3M attention model in fp16 runs on ANE, while a 25.9M conv-heavy fp16 model does not; yet the same conv graph in int8 or 2-bit reaches roughly 83% on-ANE residency. This shows that parameter count is not the governing variable. On decode cost, hardware counters measure about 1.539 / 0.753 / 0.197 bytes per parameter for fp16 / int8 / 2-bit, versus nominal 2 / 1 / 0.25, indicating real-system packing and runtime effects but confirming that streamed weight bytes dominate. On operator size, heavily grouped GQA with 2 KV heads is around 2.5d^2, whereas the LIV conv family is around 4d^2, and the grouped-attention family is admitted at fp16 while the conv-heavy family is the one that falls off ANE. On the end-to-end frontier, ternary models occupy the best size/speed tradeoff among the tested artifacts: around 25M ternary models span roughly 10.0–10.8 MB and 0.62–0.64 ms across operator mixes, while 50M ternary models are about 16.8 MB and 0.86 ms. Relative to the conv-heavy fp16 starting point, the reported gains are 9.8×/6.1× smaller and 3.0×/2.2× faster. An important secondary observation is that once encoding is pushed to ternary, operator mix matters much less for size and speed than the encoding choice itself.2a:T4e9,Read this paper as an evidence chain rather than a benchmark story. Start with the introduction and Table 1 to fix the five headline claims. Then go to §4.1 first: the primitive sweep is the cleanest part of the paper and the best place to understand why the authors insist that expression form, not semantic equivalence, controls placement. Next read §5.1 and §5.2 together. §5.1 explains why matched trained artifacts matter; §5.2 explains why runtime byte counters are necessary and how cross-chip telemetry calibration can fail silently. After that, focus on §6–§8, which map to placement, bytes-per-token, and the operator lever. The figures showing architecture × size × encoding interactions and the relationship between bytes/param and latency are the ones to study carefully. If time is limited, keep two questions in mind: why does the paper recommend choosing encoding before parameter budget, and why does grouped attention become the smaller operator family on ANE. The deeper follow-up questions are whether compiler-admissible graph forms can be enforced during training, whether byte-dominated decode should change how low-bit models are pretrained, and how much of this transfers to other NPUs rather than remaining ANE-specific.2b:T5f0,The central idea is predictive delta prefetching. First, the system predicts downstream sparsity early. Instead of using per-layer predictors that reveal a layer’s active set only when execution reaches that layer, NeuroPrefetcher runs a single GPU-resident predictor after layer 0 and predicts active-neuron masks for all downstream MLP layers in one shot from early-token features. This creates a lookahead I/O schedule before sparse execution begins. Second, the runtime converts predictions into movement decisions. For each layer it tracks which weight rows are already resident, compares that resident set with the predicted active set for the next token, and fetches only the incoming delta from NVMe. This reframes the I/O cost from “how many rows are needed now” to “how many new rows are needed relative to the previous token.” Third, the implementation addresses a platform-specific coherence issue on Jetson-style unified-memory systems: direct I/O can bypass CPU cache coherence and expose stale data to the GPU, so sparse rows are routed through a cache-coherent staging path before execution. Fourth, the runtime uses a memory-adaptive dense/sparse split. Limited GPU memory is divided between a fully resident dense prefix and a sparse suffix with active-row buffers. With more memory, more early layers stay dense; with less memory, the sparse suffix expands to preserve model executability. The design is therefore not just sparse execution, but a coordinated predictor-runtime-memory policy.2c:T4fd,The most important evidence starts with system profiling. On a Jetson AGX Orin running Mistral-7B with a 14G memory setting, decoding triggers about 11,453 major page faults per second; the CPU spends 77% of its time blocked on I/O, while the GPU averages only 3% utilization. This establishes that the bottleneck is reactive weight movement rather than arithmetic throughput. The enabling observation is also quantified clearly: around 82–85% of active MLP neurons persist across adjacent tokens. The experimental story appears to have three main parts: predictor quality and lookahead timing; end-to-end gains of delta prefetching over demand paging and prior sparse runtimes; and the trade-off induced by the dense/sparse split under different memory budgets. The introduction names relevant baselines such as llama.cpp with mmap plus demand paging, PowerInfer, LLM in a Flash, and Neuralink-style placement ideas. For a reader, the most useful results are likely not a single average speedup number, but the breakdowns showing reduced page faults, lower I/O stall time, smaller NVMe traffic, better GPU utilization, and improved decode latency. The provided full-text excerpt does not include the final result tables, so it would be unsafe to invent exact speedup values.2d:T6db,The reported effects are large. A model’s accuracy becomes a band rather than a point: gemma4-31b ranges from 31% to 89% on the same weights and the same items under different configurations. Across the roster, up to 85% of credited answers can be flipped to wrong by another defensible configuration. Adjacent-model rankings are not identified by stable items. Among 11 adjacent pairs, on items both models answer stably, 5 pairs are identical item by item, 4 differ on exactly one item, and the remaining 2 have disagreements that cancel to zero. Yet the full benchmark still orders every one of the 10 pairs with a nonzero full-set gap. Config-fragile items carry 95.7% of a pair’s gap on average, rising to 99.7% when pooling items rather than averaging pairs. Harness choice also changes winners: 4 of 12 models reach rank one under some configuration, and 8 of 12 reach the top three. mixtral-8x7b is 11th under the reference harness and 1st under another. The two models that win 24 of 26 configurations have median leads of 181.5 and 98.5 items out of 3,679; the other two single-configuration winners lead by only 1 and 3 items. Benchmark compression does not solve the issue. Item discrimination correlates with config-fragility at 0.28 with a 95% CI of 0.25 to 0.30, and the most discriminative items have mean fragility 0.96 versus 0.85 over the full benchmark. When compressed to 100 items, the champion count rises from 4 on the full benchmark to 5.1 for random subsets and 5.7 for discrimination-selected subsets, with a seeded tie-break range from 4 to 7. The scoring axis is especially important: holding other axes fixed, mean accuracy robust to scoring choice is 0.31, compared with 0.40 for prompt format and 0.60 for option order.2e:T74c,The core contribution is GUI-Primitives, a 994-item benchmark organized as 497 contrastive instruction pairs over seven GUI spatial primitives: left/right, above/below, containment, alignment, proximity, list ordinal, and occlusion. In each pair, the screenshot and anchor element stay fixed while the relation expression changes, so the correct target flips between two designated candidates. This minimal-pair construction is the key idea. It suppresses screenshot salience, centrality bias, and fixed-answer shortcuts: a model that always clicks the more salient element can only get one item in the pair correct.

The dataset also includes lexical variation beyond the relation term in 253 of the 497 pairs, changing verbs, head nouns, or anchor quoting, which reduces template memorization. A 196-item subset is validated by five annotators, with Fleiss kappa = 0.94 for well-formedness and kappa = 0.79 for target selection, giving the benchmark a reasonably strong annotation foundation.

Methodologically, the paper goes beyond strict point-in-box accuracy and performs candidate-level analysis. Errors are split into two types: selecting the contrastive wrong candidate versus predicting a point outside both candidate regions. This distinction matters because it changes the diagnosis. A low score does not necessarily mean the model misunderstood the relation word; it may simply have failed to localize either candidate reliably.

The paper also evaluates three training-free interventions: Set-of-Mark prompting, primitive-aware chain-of-thought, and activation steering. SoM explicitly marks the two candidates and therefore acts as an oracle upper bound on candidate discrimination rather than a deployable solution. CoT tests whether explicit reasoning helps. Activation steering tests whether internal representations can be nudged without retraining.2f:T84b,The empirical picture is consistent and fairly stark.

First, absolute performance is poor. Across 19 VLMs, the best model, Claude Opus 4.7, reaches only 31% strict point-in-box accuracy on the human-clean subset; the abstract also states that no model exceeds 32% overall. So even on tightly controlled GUI spatial primitives, current models remain far from human-level grounding.

Second, all tested models fall below the two-candidate reference level of 0.50 on four primitives: containment, occlusion, alignment, and proximity. Candidate-level analysis explains why: the dominant error is not choosing the opposite candidate, but predicting outside both candidate boxes. Depending on the model, off-candidate predictions account for 60% to 92% of errors, and on the real desktop screenshot subset this rises to 96.2%. The main bottleneck therefore looks more like candidate localization failure than relation-word confusion.

Third, the benchmark tracks downstream grounding. For the ten models also evaluated on ScreenSpot-Pro, GUI-Primitives accuracy correlates with downstream grounding accuracy at Spearman rho = +0.74 with p = 0.015. The paper also reports item-level logistic regression over n = 15,810 model-item pairs with pseudo-R^2 = 0.40, which supports the claim that the benchmark is diagnostically meaningful rather than synthetic in a disconnected way.

Fourth, only Set-of-Mark helps substantially. Marking the two candidates improves selection accuracy by 35 to 57 points, raising GPT-5 from 30% to 87% and OS-Atlas from 10% to 52%. Primitive-aware CoT and activation steering do not produce significant gains. This is a useful diagnosis: once the candidate set is made explicit, models can often discriminate between candidates much better than their raw screenshot-grounding scores suggest.

Fifth, shortcut controls behave mostly as expected. Blank canvas, shuffled instructions, and heavy blur reduce performance, indicating that the benchmark is not solved by pure template matching. The blur result is significant but not catastrophic, which suggests some models still rely on coarse visual cues.30:T409,Its value for foundation-model research is less about the task label and more about the interface it exposes between external memory and generation. The benchmark is a clean testbed for studying why standard RAG, static memory injection, and plain chain-of-thought break when the external knowledge is procedural rather than factual. This matters for pretraining-adjacent research because future systems will likely rely on external memory that must be queried during decoding, not only on parametric knowledge. DynaRule is interesting because it turns retrieval into an internal learned process: rules are injected into KV cache, and a special token triggers step-wise re-attention and memory refresh. That is relevant to test-time memory, retrieval-trigger tokens, and training schemes that teach models to call external knowledge incrementally. The benchmark contribution feels more durable than the method itself; the method shows a clear gain, but still reads more like a strong research prototype than a settled general recipe.31:T7e4,There are two parts: the benchmark and the model. RuleWorld contains 4.94M rules spanning four major types and seven subtypes, with both first-order logic and natural-language forms. It defines Single-Rule QA, Parallel Multi-Rule QA, and Multi-Hop Rule QA, yielding 11 sub-tasks. The benchmark design matters for three reasons. First, rules are abstract templates with placeholders, so the model must ground them before use. Second, the rule repository is shared globally rather than attached per question. Third, rules are compositional, so earlier conclusions alter what becomes relevant later.

DynaRule injects encoded external rules directly into the LLM’s KV cache and makes re-retrieval part of decoding. Rules are first embedded by a pretrained sentence encoder, then mapped into the LLM space with a lightweight adapter, and finally exposed as external memory in the KV cache. Training has two stages. In stage one, the method identifies a confidence layer, defined as the layer where attention entropy over injected rules is minimized. The intuition is that this layer exhibits the sharpest and most decisive rule selection. In stage two, the model performs Stacked Step-Level Attention Training on that layer using a special <search> token. Emitting <search> triggers re-attention over the rule memory, selects a new subset of relevant rules, and replaces outdated entries in the KV cache.

This differs from standard RAG, where retrieval is front-loaded and retrieval errors propagate through the whole generation, and from static memory injection, where the model can get stuck with early-selected rules even when later reasoning steps require different ones. Mechanistically, <search> acts like an explicit latent action: it externalizes the decision that the model needs fresh procedural support, and supervision shapes the attention pattern associated with that action. That is more interesting than adding a separate retrieval head, because it is integrated into the token-level generation trajectory.32:T77f,The empirical message is clear: RuleWorld is hard for current strong models, especially with large rule sets and multi-step reasoning. The headline numbers reported in the paper are substantial. DynaRule improves average QA accuracy by up to 19 points across rule-injection settings, and by as much as 48 points on some sub-tasks. On retrieval, at 10K rules it achieves over 90% Recall@10 and over 85% Recall@1, beating the strongest baseline by more than 60 points.

The more important part is how the experiments are structured. The paper compares RAG-style baselines, internal injection baselines, and the proposed dynamic update mechanism. When rules are abstract and only weakly aligned with the query surface form, text retrieval becomes brittle. When tasks require parallel aggregation or multi-hop state tracking, one-shot injection degrades sharply because the relevant rule set changes across steps. Ablations show that the confidence-layer choice, the <search> token, and step-level attention training all matter. Removing dynamic re-attention or skipping step-wise supervision causes clear drops. Another useful observation is scaling behavior: as the rule repository grows, baseline performance degrades faster, while DynaRule degrades more gracefully. That suggests the gain comes from stable narrowing of attention over a large candidate set, not merely from adding extra memory.

There are also limits. The strongest results are obtained in a synthetic rule world with controlled semantics and relatively formal structure. This is enough to demonstrate feasibility for procedural knowledge use, but it is still some distance from real policy documents, noisy enterprise rules, or conflicting cross-document regulations. A second likely bottleneck is the rule encoder plus adapter. If rules become longer, noisier, or more context-dependent, the current setup may not preserve the same retrieval quality.33:T46c,For a skim pass, start with the introduction and Figure 1 to understand why the benchmark is harder than premise-given rule QA. Then jump to the method overview and the training pipeline. Four parts deserve attention. First, the benchmark construction: shared repository, abstract placeholders, and compositional rules are the real source of difficulty. Second, the definition of the confidence layer, because it reveals the authors’ view of where external rule selection should happen inside the network. Third, the exact role of the <search> token in re-attention and KV replacement; that is the core mechanism. Fourth, read the QA tables together with the retrieval tables, otherwise it is hard to separate retrieval failure from reasoning failure.

A useful skim should take around 20 to 30 minutes: introduction, method figure, main tables, and key ablations. If reading more carefully, two questions are worth tracking. How is supervision for <search> constructed, and does it introduce teacher-forcing bias? And how exactly is KV replacement implemented, especially when previously selected rules may still be useful later?34:T804,The work has three main components. First, it formalizes Online Video Question Answering under Interruption. A user asks an initial question at time tq, the model starts autoregressive generation, and an interruption arrives at time ti. The observable context is the partial video V≤ti, the original question Q0, the partial answer A≤ti, and the interruption signal I. The model must jointly perform interruption-type recognition and response generation. The interruption taxonomy has three classes—Cancellation, False Trigger, and Correction—defined by whether the interruption changes the semantic context and whether it requires a change in generation strategy. That is a more meaningful decomposition than a simple stop/continue label.

Second, the paper builds OVIBench and an offline simulation protocol. The key practical challenge is reproducibility: true online interruption during generation is hard to benchmark at scale. The proposed protocol freezes a unified temporal state—what video has been seen, what has been generated, and when the interruption occurs—so models can be evaluated in a controlled and repeatable way. The benchmark supports both open-ended and multiple-choice settings. Open-ended evaluation is closer to real interaction, while multiple-choice reduces evaluation noise and makes model comparison more stable.

Third, the paper introduces a metric suite and a training set. Metrics cover interruption understanding and response generation, including interruption-intent recognition, post-interruption intent fulfillment, fluency and consistency before and after interruption, and alignment with video evidence. To test whether the benchmark can support improvement rather than only diagnosis, the authors construct OVI-Train, a multiple-choice training set for interruption-aware fine-tuning. The design choice is pragmatic: use a structured format to teach the model the chain from interruption recognition to response strategy, instead of relying only on unconstrained generation to absorb this behavior.35:T518,The main empirical result is straightforward: mainstream MLLMs that perform well on standard offline VideoQA degrade substantially under interruption. The weakest case is Correction, where the model must revise or override an ongoing answer rather than simply continue or stop. That pattern suggests the failure is not only in visual grounding, but in updating intent and rewriting generation policy mid-trajectory.

The most informative quantitative result comes from targeted fine-tuning. Starting from Qwen2.5-VL-7B, the interruption-aware fine-tuned 7B model improves multiple-choice accuracy by 16.88% and interruption-type accuracy by 13.94%, and even surpasses the 72B model in the same series. That is a strong indication that interruption handling is highly data- and objective-sensitive. Larger scale alone does not reliably induce the behavior if the model never sees the right supervision.

The paper also contrasts open-ended and multiple-choice evaluation. Open-ended testing is more realistic but noisier; multiple-choice is cleaner for controlled comparison and for closing the train-eval loop. If the benchmark is used as a diagnostic tool, the multiple-choice setting is especially useful because it helps separate failures in video understanding from failures in interruption handling.36:T664,The core idea is to replace absolute scoring of a single review with anonymized pairwise battles plus structured dimension-wise judgments. The platform has four main design choices. First, annotators are not crowd workers; they are domain experts with AI paper-writing experience. The motivation is straightforward: judging review structure, field organization, and research directions requires field-level judgment. Second, the prompts are not synthetic topics. They are derived from real, highly cited surveys so that the tasks correspond to recognizable and academically meaningful review problems. Third, the platform performs expertise matching, assigning topics to reviewers within their area, which makes the setup closer to actual peer review. Fourth, the evaluation is decomposed into five literature-review-specific dimensions: D1 literature coverage, D2 claim support, D3 paper structure, D4 research suggestions quality, and D5 overall utility.

What distinguishes this from generic arena evaluation is that the battle format is not just a scaling trick for preference collection. It is used to operationalize subjective scientific judgment in a more disciplined way. The paper also adds a second layer: using LitReviewBench as calibration data for a low-cost evaluator. The authors show that off-the-shelf LLM-as-a-judge is substantially misaligned with expert preferences and can produce incorrect system rankings. Instead of treating the judge as a drop-in replacement for experts, they explicitly supervise or calibrate it with expert preference data. That is a more careful and more useful framing for evaluator design.37:T4fe,There are four headline results. First, the benchmark contains about 3k expert judgments, each with outcomes on all five dimensions rather than only an overall preference. Second, in decisive matches against human-written drafts, even the strongest current systems achieve only a 23.0% win rate on D5 overall utility, which indicates a large remaining gap to human performance. Third, the largest deficits appear on D3 paper structure and D4 research suggestions, with models trailing humans by roughly 200 rating points. This is an important diagnosis: the bottleneck is not just retrieval or citation accuracy, but coherent field organization and non-generic research insight. Fourth, agentic systems outperform pure language models across dimensions, but they do so at a substantial inference-time cost, averaging about 15× the token budget for the same topic.

The evaluator-calibration result is also practically important. Uncalibrated LLM judges correlate poorly with expert rankings; on D5 the reported alignment is only around ρ≈0.467. After calibration using LitReviewBench, the correlation rises to about ρ≈0.792. For people working on reward modeling, evaluator design, or scalable preference supervision, this is one of the most useful findings in the paper.38:T402,Repo2Skill-Evo is built as:
1) A fixed V1 skill set produced by Repo2Skill via staged distillation plus expert refinement. Each skill is packaged (e.g., SKILL.md, optional scripts/references) and augmented with provenance metadata (source paths/symbols) to enable traceability.
2) A maintenance task: given the official V1→V2 release patch, a maintenance agent must delete or revise patch-verified obsolete skill lines while preserving still-valid content. The design intentionally emphasizes minimal, selective edits rather than full regeneration.
3) Evaluation: a strict patch-grounded headline metric that rewards correct edits on verified obsolete lines and penalizes edits elsewhere, plus a rubric-based NL judge with five dimensions for overall quality.
The analysis frames two complementary bottlenecks: incomplete localization of affected skill files (coverage) and imperfect edit selection (precision/within-file evidence tracing). An oracle localization experiment tests how much of the gap is purely localization.39:T828,KTC combines predictive uncertainty and semantic contradiction at the token level through a kernel construction, then scores that kernel with Von Neumann entropy. The intuition is straightforward. Standard token entropy only says that the model is uncertain; it does not tell whether the competing tokens correspond to equivalent phrasings or to conflicting factual content. The authors argue that the latter is much more indicative of hallucination risk.

The method has four main steps. First, at each next-token prediction step, KTC considers candidate tokens and estimates pairwise contradiction scores between them. Unlike methods such as CCP or SAR, it does not use an external NLI model. Instead, it introduces a heuristic based on neighbor-frequency statistics from Wikipedia. The idea is to use local corpus statistics to approximate whether two candidate tokens represent incompatible continuations in context. This is clearly a rougher proxy than full semantic inference, but it is extremely cheap.

Second, these contradiction relations are used to build a positive semi-definite kernel over candidate tokens, representing the semantic disagreement structure among plausible continuations. Third, KTC scales semantic uncertainty by the model’s predictive distribution. This is an important design choice: if contradictory tokens have negligible probability mass, they should not dominate the uncertainty score. The paper emphasizes that this scaling step is critical at the token level and is one of the main departures from generation-level KLE. Fourth, the normalized kernel is scored with Von Neumann entropy, and token-level scores are then aggregated to the claim level.

Methodologically, KTC sits between plain predictive entropy and heavier semantic verification pipelines. It borrows the PSD-kernel plus VNE machinery from KLE, but moves it from whole-generation uncertainty to token-level uncertainty, and replaces NLI-based contradiction detection with a corpus-statistics heuristic. The trade-off is clear: less semantic precision, much lower computational cost.3a:T5e6,The evaluation uses two claim-level UQ benchmarks, MUCH and Mu-SHROOM, across four languages: English, French, German, and Spanish. The experiments cover 16 LLMs from multiple families and compare KTC against comparable white-box baselines, especially CCP and SAR.

The main empirical picture is balanced. In overall average performance, CCP remains the strongest method, while KTC ranks second with only a small gap. So KTC does not win by raw aggregate score. Its main advantage is efficiency and operating point. The paper reports at least an 8.2x speedup relative to state-of-the-art GPU-accelerated methods, while running CPU-only and requiring no extra NLI model. More importantly, KTC performs best in the high-precision regime: at recall less than or equal to 20%, it reaches 78% average precision, compared with 69% for CCP. That matters if the goal is to flag only the most suspicious claims with minimal false alarms.

The ablations also matter. The paper highlights that scaling semantic uncertainty by the predictive distribution is crucial for token-level performance; removing that step hurts results noticeably. Another practical takeaway is that the Wikipedia-based contradiction heuristic, while simple, is strong enough to serve as a useful replacement for NLI in many settings. At the same time, its limits are easy to see: it should work better for local conflicts such as numbers, entities, or short substitutions than for long-range semantic contradiction or complex entailment patterns.3b:T443,For a skim read, start with the introduction and the method overview figure. The key question is whether token contradiction really captures something that plain entropy misses. Then focus on two design choices: how the Wikipedia neighbor-frequency heuristic approximates contradiction, and why semantic uncertainty must be weighted by the model’s predictive probabilities. Those are the two places where the method either stands as a coherent approach or collapses into a bag of tricks.

In the experiments, prioritize the main comparison against CCP and SAR, and especially the high-precision results. KTC’s case is not that it dominates average performance, but that it is much cheaper and stronger in precision-first settings. If time is limited, at least read the ablation on the scaling step. A practical skim should take around 20 minutes. If you work on generation monitoring, confidence modeling, or filtering pipelines, this is a useful lightweight white-box baseline. If your focus is pretraining objectives, the method intuition and the main result table are probably enough.3c:T410,1) Dataset design: 500 handwritten document images spanning three structures (free text, tables, formulas), four language settings (mostly Simplified Chinese plus English, Traditional Chinese, and mixed CN-EN), and nine real-world scenarios. It also provides per-sample difficulty annotations along six axes (e.g., degradation, cursiveness, structural complexity).
2) Structured ground truth: Markdown for free text, LaTeX for formulas, and HTML tables for tabular content—pushing evaluation from token/character correctness toward layout/structure fidelity.
3) PDE (Prior-Driven Error): an error attribution metric intended to quantify whether a model’s mistake is better explained by language priors than by the visual evidence, capturing confident but unsupported “plausible” transcriptions.
4) Evaluation protocol: 18 contemporary MLLMs / OCR-oriented VLMs are tested under a unified setup, alongside calibrated human baselines, enabling qualitative comparison of error types (humans being conservative vs models hallucinating).3d:T90d,Methodologically, this is a survey, but not a standard taxonomy survey. The authors first narrow the scope to the generative route of world models: models that map current state and action to a distribution over future observations or states. Policy-route systems are discussed as context, but not treated as simulator substitutes because their interface is fundamentally different from a state-feedback simulator. They then define an eight-capability framework: C1 asset construction, C2 physics engine, C3 interaction, C4 controllability, C5 stability, C6 state feedback, C7 diversity, and C8 evaluation metrics. This framing asks what responsibilities a model can actually assume in a simulator stack, rather than what model family it belongs to.

The main analytical innovations are threefold. First, the benchmark is external: traditional simulator capabilities, not self-referential world-model categories. That makes the gap between “looks plausible” and “simulates correctly” much easier to expose. Second, the paper uses paper-level coding to build a reproducible evidence map rather than a purely narrative review. Third, it treats state feedback as a first-class capability and quantifies it with a B1–B5 labeling scheme. This is one of the strongest parts of the paper. Many world models can emit pixels, tokens, or latent states, but they do not provide explicit object states, physical variables, queryable interfaces, or structured feedback that planners, controllers, or verifiers can reliably consume.
